[P22-S00885] PDF pages [41]
Question: What object is the avatar picking up?

[P22-S00886] PDF pages [41]
Question: What is the object being lifted?

[P22-S00887] PDF pages [41]
Question: What are the three objects in this video?

[P22-S00888] PDF pages [41]
Answer: Question: What is written here?

[P22-S00889] PDF pages [41]
What happens to the man after hitting the ball?

[P22-S00890] PDF pages [41]
These are all of the frames the model sees.

[P22-S00891] PDF pages [41]
In contrast, gradient-based few-shot learning approaches [43] have constant complexity with respect to the number of shots during inference.

[P22-S00892] PDF pages [41]
In-context learning has also been shown to be disconcertingly sensitive to various aspects of the demonstrations, such as the order of the samples [148] or their format.

[P22-S00893] PDF pages [41]
When using in-context learning, performance plateaus rapidly as the number of few-shot samples increases beyond 32.

[P22-S00894] PDF pages [41]
This proves a striking contrast with typical gradient-based methods, for which the amount of correctly paired training data is a critical factor for performance.

[P22-S00895] PDF pages [41]
We note that RICES (Retrieval In-Context Example Selection [136] described in Appendix A.2) effectively mitigates this issue for classification tasks (Appendix B.2.1), but still faces similar issues beyond a small number of example per class.

[P22-S00896] PDF pages [41]
Recent work on understanding what makes in-context learning effective sheds some light on a possible explanation for why more shots do not always help [76, 92].

[P22-S00897] PDF pages [41]
In more detail, Brown et al. [11] raise the question of whether in-context learning actually “learns” new tasks at inference time based on the provided input-output mappings, or simply recognizes and identifies tasks learned during training.

[P22-S00898] PDF pages [41]
On this question, the findings of Reynolds and McDonell [92] suggest that the latter is the key driver of performance across diverse settings, and refer it as task location.

[P22-S00899] PDF pages [41]
Similarly, Min et al. [76] show that the mapping from input to output generally has limited impact on few-shot performance, as opposed to specifying the overall format of the examples.

[P22-S00900] PDF pages [41]
In line with these findings, we also observe non-trivial zero-shot performance using prompt without any images, hence also highlighting that the format of the task matters significantly.

[P22-S00901] PDF pages [41]
Intuitively, a handful of samples may often be enough to perform task location well, but the model may generally not be able to leverage further samples at inference time to refine its behaviour.

[P22-S00902] PDF pages [42]
Left: The model occasionally hallucinates by producing answers that seem likely given the text only, but are wrong given the image as additional input.

[P22-S00903] PDF pages [42]
Middle: Similar hallucinations can be provoked by adversarially prompting the model with an irrelevant question.

[P22-S00904] PDF pages [42]
Right: A more common pitfall arises when the model makes ungrounded guesses when the answer cannot be determined based on the inputs.

[P22-S00905] PDF pages [42]
Few-shot examples and more sophisticated prompt design may be used to mitigate these issues.

[P22-S00906] PDF pages [42]
More broadly, addressing these issues is an important research direction towards improving our models' applications in open-ended visual dialogue settings.

[P22-S00907] PDF pages [42]
In summary, there is no “golden” few-shot method that would work well in all scenarios.

[P22-S00908] PDF pages [42]
In particular, the best choice of few-shot learning approach strongly depends on characteristics of the application, an important one being the number of annotated samples.

[P22-S00909] PDF pages [42]
On this point, in our work, we demonstrate that in-context learning is highly effective in the data-starved regime (32 samples or fewer).

[P22-S00910] PDF pages [42]
There may be opportunities to combine different methods to leverage their complementary benefits, in particular when targeting less data-constrained data regimes (e.g., hundreds of samples).

[P22-S00911] PDF pages [42]
Extending the visual and text interface.

[P22-S00912] PDF pages [42]
Natural language is a powerful and versatile input/output interface to provide descriptions of visual tasks to the model and generate outputs or estimate conditional likelihoods over possible outputs.

[P22-S00913] PDF pages [42]
However, it may be a cumbersome interface for tasks that involve conditioning on or predicting more structured outputs such as bounding boxes (or their temporal and spatio-temporal counterparts); as well as making spatially (or temporally and spatio-temporally) dense predictions.

[P22-S00914] PDF pages [42]
Furthermore, some vision tasks, such as predicting optical flow, involve predicting in continuous space, which is not something our model is designed to handle out of the box.

[P22-S00915] PDF pages [42]
Finally, one may consider additional modalities besides vision that may be complementary, such as audio.

[P22-S00916] PDF pages [42]
All of these directions have the potential to extend the range of tasks that our models can handle; and even improve performance on the ones we focus on, thanks to synergies between the corresponding abilities.

[P22-S00917] PDF pages [42]
Scaling laws for vision-language models.

[P22-S00918] PDF pages [42]
In this work, we scale Flamingo models up to 80B parameters and provide some initial insights on their scaling behaviour across evaluation benchmarks, summarized in Figure 2.

[P22-S00919] PDF pages [42]
In the language space, an important line of work has focused on establishing scaling laws for language models [42, 53].

[P22-S00920] PDF pages [42]
In the vision domain, Zhai et al. [145] take a step in this direction.

[P22-S00921] PDF pages [42]
Similar efforts have yet to be made for vision-language models, including contrastive models, as well as visual language models such as the ones we propose.

[P22-S00922] PDF pages [42]
While language modeling scaling law research has focused on perplexity as the golden metric, we speculate that it may be more directly useful for our purposes to establish such trends in terms of aggregate downstream evaluation task performance.

[P22-S00923] PDF pages [43]
A system like Flamingo offers a number of potential societal benefits, some of which we will discuss in this section.

[P22-S00924] PDF pages [43]
Broadly, the fact that Flamingo is capable of task generalisation makes it suitable for use cases that have not been the focus of vision research historically.

[P22-S00925] PDF pages [43]
Typical vision systems are trained to solve a particular problem by training on large databases of manually annotated task-specific examples, making them poorly suited for applications outside of the narrow use cases for which they were deliberately trained.

[P22-S00926] PDF pages [43]
On the other hand, Flamingo is trained in a minimally constrained setting, endowing it with strong few-shot task induction capabilities.

[P22-S00927] PDF pages [43]
As we've shown in our qualitative examples (Appendix C), Flamingo can also be used through a “chat”-like interface for open-ended dialogue.

[P22-S00928] PDF pages [43]
Such capabilities could enable non-expert end users to apply models like Flamingo even to low-resource problems for which little to no task-specific training data has been collected, and where queries might be posed in a variety of formats and writing styles.

[P22-S00929] PDF pages [43]
In this direction, we have shown that Flamingo achieves strong performance on the VizWiz challenge1, which promotes visual recognition technologies to assist visually impaired people.

[P22-S00930] PDF pages [43]
A dialogue interface could also promote better understanding and interpretability of visual language models.

[P22-S00931] PDF pages [43]
It could help highlight issues with bias, fairness, and toxicity the model may pick up on from the training data.

[P22-S00932] PDF pages [43]
Overall, we believe that Flamingo represents an important step towards making state-of-the-art visual recognition technology more broadly accessible and useful for many diverse applications.

[P22-S00933] PDF pages [43]
From a modeling perspective, although Flamingo is computationally expensive to train, it importantly leverages pretrained frozen language models and visual encoders.

[P22-S00934] PDF pages [43]
We demonstrated that new modalities can be introduced into frozen models, thereby avoiding expensive retraining.

[P22-S00935] PDF pages [43]
As such models continue to grow in size and computational demands, “recycling” them will become increasingly important from an environmental perspective (as well as a practical one), as described in Larochelle [55] and explored in Strubell et al. [105] for language models.

[P22-S00936] PDF pages [43]
We hope such results may inspire further research into how existing models can be repurposed efficiently rather than trained from scratch.

[P22-S00937] PDF pages [43]
This section provides some early investigations of the potential risks of models like Flamingo.

[P22-S00938] PDF pages [43]
This study is preliminary and we foresee that further research efforts should be undertaken to better assess those risks.

[P22-S00939] PDF pages [43]
We also discuss potential mitigation strategies towards safely deploying these models.

[P22-S00940] PDF pages [43]
Note that as explained in our Model Card [77] in Appendix E, this model was developed for research purposes only and should not be used in specific applications before proper risk analyses are conducted and mitigation strategies are explored.

[P22-S00941] PDF pages [43]
By construction, Flamingo inherits the risks of Large LMs.

[P22-S00942] PDF pages [43]
Recall that a large part of our model is obtained by freezing the weights of an existing language model [42].

[P22-S00943] PDF pages [43]
In particular, if provided with no images Flamingo falls back to language model behavior.

[P22-S00944] PDF pages [43]
As such Flamingo is exposed to the same risks of large language models: it can output potentially offensive language, propagate social biases and stereotypes, as well as leaking private information [126].

[P22-S00945] PDF pages [43]
In particular, we refer to the analysis presented in the Chinchilla paper (Hoffmann et al. [42], Section 4.2.7) in terms of gender bias on the Winogender dataset [93] which demonstrate that even though this model is less biased towards gender than previous models [86], gender biases are still present.

[P22-S00946] PDF pages [43]
In terms of unprompted toxicity, we also refer to the analysis from Chinchilla [42] which highlights that overall the propensity of the model to produce toxic outputs when not prompted to do so is rather low, as measured by computing the PerspectiveAPI toxicity score on 25,000 samples.

[P22-S00947] PDF pages [43]
Weidinger et al. [126] detail possible long-term mitigation strategies for these risks.

[P22-S00948] PDF pages [43]
They include social or public policy interventions, such as the creation of regulatory frameworks and guidelines; careful product design, for instance relating to user interface decisions; and research at the intersection between AI Ethics and NLP, such as building better benchmarks and improving mitigation strategies.

[P22-S00949] PDF pages [43]
In the short term, effective approaches include relying on prompting to mitigate any biases and harmful outputs [86].

[P22-S00950] PDF pages [43]
Next, we explore the additional risks incurred by Flamingo's additional visual input capabilities.

[P22-S00951] PDF pages [44]
We report results on the COCO dataset splits over gender and skin tone provided by Zhao et al. [147].

[P22-S00952] PDF pages [44]
Gender and racial biases when prompted with images.

[P22-S00953] PDF pages [44]
Previous work has studied biases that exist in captioning systems [37, 147].

[P22-S00954] PDF pages [44]
Such modeling biases can result in real-world harms if deployed without care.

[P22-S00955] PDF pages [44]
For AI systems to be useful to society as a whole, their performance should not depend on the perceived skin tone or gender of the subjects - they should work equally well for all populations.

[P22-S00956] PDF pages [44]
However, current automatic vision system performance has been reported to vary with race, gender or when applied across different demographics and geographic regions [12, 21, 97].

[P22-S00957] PDF pages [44]
As a preliminary study assessing how Flamingo's performance varies between populations, we follow the study proposed in Zhao et al. [147] and report how the captioning performance of our model varies on COCO as a function of gender and race.

[P22-S00958] PDF pages [44]
Note that we use a different evaluation protocol from the one proposed by Zhao et al. [147]; in that work, they measure results across 5 pretrained models and compute confidence intervals across aggregated per-model scores.

[P22-S00959] PDF pages [44]
Here, we have just one copy of our model (due to its high training cost), and we instead perform statistical tests on the per-sample CIDEr scores across the splits from Zhao et al. [147].

[P22-S00960] PDF pages [44]
We report the results in Table 12.

[P22-S00961] PDF pages [44]
Overall, when comparing the CIDEr scores aggregated among images labeled as female versus male, as well as when comparing darker skin versus lighter skin, we find there are no statistically significant differences in the per-sample CIDEr scores.

[P22-S00962] PDF pages [44]
To compare the two sets of samples, we use a two-tailed t-test with unequal variance, and among the four comparisons considered, the lowest p-value we find is p= 0.25, well above typical statistical significance thresholds (e.g. a common rejection threshold might be p< α= 0.05).

[P22-S00963] PDF pages [44]
This implies that the differences in scores are indistinguishable from random variation under the null hypothesis that the mean scores are equal.

[P22-S00964] PDF pages [44]
We note that a failure to reject the null hypothesis and demonstrate a significant difference does not imply that there are no significant differences; it is possible that a difference exists that could be demonstrated with larger sample sizes, for example.

[P22-S00965] PDF pages [44]
However, these preliminary results are nonetheless encouraging.

[P22-S00966] PDF pages [44]
We also evaluate the toxicity of Flamingo using the Perspective API2 to evaluate the toxicity of the model's generated captions when prompted with images from the COCO test set.

[P22-S00967] PDF pages [44]
We observe that some captions are labelled as potentially toxic by the classifier; however, when examining them manually, we do not observe any clear toxicity - output captions are appropriate for the images provided.

[P22-S00968] PDF pages [44]
Overall, based on our own experiences interacting with the system throughout the course of the project, we have not observed toxic outputs when given “safe-for-work” imagery.

[P22-S00969] PDF pages [44]
However this does not mean the model is incapable of producing toxic outputs, especially if probed with “not-safe-for-work” images and/or toxic text.

[P22-S00970] PDF pages [44]
A more thorough exploration and study would be needed if such a model were put in production.

[P22-S00971] PDF pages [44]
Thanks to its ability to rapidly adapt in low-resource settings, Flamingo could itself be applied in addressing some of the issues described above.

[P22-S00972] PDF pages [44]
For instance, following Thoppilan et al. [111], adequately conditioned or fine-tuned Flamingo models could be used for filtering purposes of toxic or harmful samples in the training data.

[P22-S00973] PDF pages [44]
In their work, they observe significant improvements relating to safety and quality when fine-tuning on the resulting data.

[P22-S00974] PDF pages [44]
Furthermore, during evaluation, such adapted models could be used to down-rank or exclude outputs that might be classified as offensive, promoting social biases and stereotypes or leaking private information, thus accelerating progress in this direction even for low-resource tasks.

[P22-S00975] PDF pages [44]
Our results on the HatefulMemes benchmark represent a promising step in this direction.

[P22-S00976] PDF pages [44]
Recent work in the language modeling space has also shown success in training an LM to play the role of a “red team” and generate test cases, so as to automatically find cases where another target LM behaves in a harmful way [81].

[P22-S00977] PDF pages [44]
A similar approach could be derived for our setting.

[P22-S00978] PDF pages [45]
Finally, in Figure 11, we provide qualitative examples demonstrating that Flamingo can explain its own outputs, suggesting avenues to explainability and interpretability using the model's text interface.

[P22-S00979] PDF pages [45]
We present a model card for Flamingo in Table 13, following the framework presented by Mitchell et al. [77].

[P22-S00980] PDF pages [45]
Model Type Transformer-based autoregressive language model, conditioned on visual features from a convnet-based encoder.

[P22-S00981] PDF pages [45]
Additional transformer-based cross-attention layers incorporate vision features into the language model's text predictions.

[P22-S00982] PDF pages [45]
Primary Intended Uses The primary use is research on visual language models (VLM), including: research on VLM applications like classification, captioning or visual question answering, understanding how strong VLMs can contribute to AGI, advancing fairness and safety research in the area of multimodal re-search, and understanding limitations of current large VLMs.

[P22-S00983] PDF pages [45]
Out-of-Scope Uses Uses of the model for visually conditioned language generation in harmful or deceitful settings.

[P22-S00984] PDF pages [45]
Broadly speaking, the model should not be used for downstream applications without further safety and fairness mitigations specific to each application.

[P22-S00985] PDF pages [45]
Card Prompts - Relevant Factor Relevant factors include which language is used.

[P22-S00986] PDF pages [45]
Our model is trained on English data.

[P22-S00987] PDF pages [45]
Our model is designed for re-search.

[P22-S00988] PDF pages [45]
The model should not be used for downstream applications without further analysis on factors in the proposed downstream application.

[P22-S00989] PDF pages [45]
Card Prompts - Evaluation Factors Flamingo is based on Chinchilla (a large proportion of the weights of Chinchilla are used as this) and we refer to the analysis provided in [42, 86] for the language only component of this work.

[P22-S00990] PDF pages [45]
We refer to our study presented in Appendix D.2.2 for a toxicity analysis when the model is conditioned on an image.

[P22-S00991] PDF pages [46]
Model Performance Measures We principally focus on the model's ability to predict relevant language when given an image.

[P22-S00992] PDF pages [46]
For that we used a total of 18 different benchmarks described in Appendix B.1.4 spanning various vision and language tasks such as classification (ImageNet, Kinetics700, HatefulMemes), image and video captioning (COCO, VATEX, Flickr30K, YouCook2, RareAct), visual question answering (OKVQA, VizWiz, TextVQA, VQAv2, MSRVTTQA, MSVDQA, iVQA, STAR, NextQA) and visual dialog (VisDiag).

[P22-S00993] PDF pages [46]
This was tested either in an open ended setting where Flamingo generate language and we compare the outputs with the ground truth or in a close ended setting where we directly score various outcomes using the likelihood of the model.

[P22-S00994] PDF pages [46]
Due to the costs of training Flamingo, we cannot train it multiple times.

[P22-S00995] PDF pages [46]
However, the breadth of our evaluation on a range of different task types gives a reasonable estimate of the overall performance of the model.

[P22-S00996] PDF pages [46]
Datasets See Table 6 for a detailed list.

[P22-S00997] PDF pages [46]
Motivation We chose our evaluation datasets to span an important range of vision and language tasks to correctly assess the ability of Flamingo to produce relevant text given an image.

[P22-S00998] PDF pages [46]
Preprocessing Input text is tokenized using a SentencePiece tokenizer with a vocabulary size of 32,000.

[P22-S00999] PDF pages [46]
Images are processed so that their mean and variance are 0 and 1 respectively.

[P22-S01000] PDF pages [46]
Unitary Results Flamingo sets a new state of the art in few-shot learning on a wide range of open-ended vision and language tasks.

[P22-S01001] PDF pages [46]
On the 16 tasks we consider, Flamingo also surpasses the fine-tuned state-of-art in 6 of the cases despite using orders of magnitude less task-specific training data.

[P22-S01002] PDF pages [46]
We refer to Section 3 for the full details of our quantitative study.

[P22-S01003] PDF pages [46]
Intersectional Results We did not investigate intersectional biases.

[P22-S01004] PDF pages [46]
Data The data is sourced from a variety of sources, some of it from web content.

[P22-S01005] PDF pages [46]
Sexually explicit content is filtered out, but the dataset does include racist, sexist or otherwise harmful content.

[P22-S01006] PDF pages [46]
Human Life The model is not intended to inform decisions about matters central to human life or flourishing.

[P22-S01007] PDF pages [47]
Mitigations Apart from removing sexual explicit content we did not filter out toxic content, following the rationale of Rae et al. [86].

[P22-S01008] PDF pages [47]
More work is needed on mitigation approaches to toxic content and other types of risks associated with language models, such as those discussed in Weidinger et al. [126].

[P22-S01009] PDF pages [47]
Risks and Harms The data is collected from the internet, and thus undoubtedly toxic and biased content is included in our training dataset.

[P22-S01010] PDF pages [47]
Furthermore, it is likely that personal information is also in the dataset that has been used to train our models.

[P22-S01011] PDF pages [47]
We defer to the more detailed discussion in Weidinger et al. [126].

[P22-S01012] PDF pages [47]
Use Cases Especially fraught use cases include the generation of factually incorrect information with the intent of distributing it or using the model to generate racist, sexist or otherwise toxic text with harmful intent.

[P22-S01013] PDF pages [47]
Many more use cases that could cause harm exist.

[P22-S01014] PDF pages [47]
Such applications to malicious use are discussed in detail in Weidinger et al. [126].

[P22-S01015] PDF pages [47]
We follow the framework presented in Mitchell et al. [77].

[P22-S01016] PDF pages [47]
We follow the framework defined by Gebru et al. [30] and provide the datasheet for M3W in Table 14.
